Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"

It feels unwanted, not like normal creative second-guessing.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you find yourself doing or thinking something to try to make these unwanted thoughts go away?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 32 / artist_or_related_worker / bachelors\n- severity: Mild\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Compulsions\nmanifestation: \"I’ll end up rereading the label text, nudging the same objects on the table, and checking the layout photos over and over until it feels ‘settled,’ even though part of me knows it’s already fine and I’m just losing studio time.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Reserved patient.\nA reserved patient may\n1) provide brief, vague, or evasive answers to questions about their symptoms and experiences,\n2) be reluctant to answer questions about personal information, emotions, or sensitive clinical experiences, and\n3) refrain from voluntarily providing information beyond what the doctor specifically asks.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"

Even though part of me knows it’s probably already fine.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you think these actions, like rereading the label or rearranging objects, are specifically to prevent something from going wrong?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 32 / artist_or_related_worker / bachelors\n- severity: Mild\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Compulsions\nmanifestation: \"I’ll end up rereading the label text, nudging the same objects on the table, and checking the layout photos over and over until it feels ‘settled,’ even though part of me knows it’s already fine and I’m just losing studio time.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Reserved patient.\nA reserved patient may\n1) provide brief, vague, or evasive answers to questions about their symptoms and experiences,\n2) be reluctant to answer questions about personal information, emotions, or sensitive clinical experiences, and\n3) refrain from voluntarily providing information beyond what the doctor specifically asks.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n15\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF42.2, F43.8\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I… I came because I’ve been having these unwanted thoughts while working on a memorial piece for my aunt. It feels like if I choose the wrong photo or color, somehow I’m dishonoring her, or it means I didn’t love her enough. I know it sounds like just creative stress, but I can’t really shake it.\n\nDoctor: Would you describe these thoughts as popping into your mind unexpectedly?\n\nPatient: Yes… they do. I’ll just be looking at the piece, trying to choose a photo or a color, and suddenly it’s like, what if this means I’m dishonoring her, or I didn’t love her enough. It feels unwanted, not like normal creative second-guessing.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself doing or thinking something to try to make these unwanted thoughts go away?\n\nPatient: Yes… I guess I do. I’ll reread the label text, move the same objects around on the table, check the layout photos again and again… just until it feels sort of “settled.” I know it’s probably already fine, but I can lose a lot of studio time doing that.\n\nDoctor: Do you think that if you didn't repeat those actions, like rereading the label text or rearranging objects, something bad would happen?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I truly think something bad would happen… not exactly. It’s more that it feels wrong if I don’t. So I end up rereading the label, nudging the same objects around, checking the layout photos again, until it feels settled. Even though part of me knows it’s probably already fine.\n\nDoctor: Do you think these actions, like rereading the label or rearranging objects, are specifically to prevent something from going wrong?\n\nPatient: Not really to prevent something, I don’t think. It’s more… I reread the label or keep moving the objects and checking the layout until it feels settled. I know it’s probably fine already, but I still end up doing it, and then I’ve lost studio time.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel less anxious after you've finished rereading the label or rearranging the objects?\n\nPatient: A little, I guess. Mostly it just feels… settled for a bit after I reread it or move things enough. But I also know I’ve spent too much time on it.\n\nDoctor: Do you do these actions to feel less anxious?\n\nPatient: I guess… maybe a little, but it’s not really like I’m trying to calm myself down. I just keep rereading the label or moving the objects until it feels settled. Then I can stop, even though I know it was probably already fine and I’ve wasted time.\n\nDoctor: Are you doing these actions mostly to make the anxious feeling go away?\n\nPatient: Mostly it’s to make it feel… settled, I guess. I’ll reread the label or nudge things on the table, check the photos again, and part of me knows it’s probably already fine. But I keep going until that feeling eases enough to stop. It does cost me studio time.\n\nDoctor: Are the actions you take, like rereading the label or moving objects, primarily to reduce your anxiety?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, not exactly “anxiety,” maybe. It’s more that I reread the label text or nudge the objects, check the layout photos again, until it feels settled. I know it’s probably already fine, but I still end up losing studio time over it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to perform these actions, like rereading the label or moving objects, even if you don't want to?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess it does feel like I have to. I don’t really want to keep doing it, but I’ll reread the label or keep nudging the same things until it feels settled. Even when I know it’s probably fine.\n\nDoctor: Are your actions, like rereading the label or moving objects, intended to achieve a perfect result rather than to prevent something bad from happening?\n\nPatient: Yeah… more like that. I’m not usually thinking something terrible will happen. It’s just the label, or the objects on the table, or the layout photos don’t feel right yet, so I keep going until it feels settled. Even though I know it’s probably fine.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your actions, such as rereading the label or moving objects, are aimed at making everything perfect?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I know it’s probably already fine, but I still end up doing it and losing time in the studio.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your actions are driven more by the need to get everything just right, rather than the fear of something going wrong if you don't?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it’s more that “just right” thing. It felt awful, and I couldn’t really just dismiss it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're trying to avoid making a mistake with the memorial piece when you reread the label or move objects around?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I’ll reread the label text, move the same objects around on the table, check the layout photos again and again… just until it feels sort of “settled.” I know it’s probably already fine, but I can lose a lot of studio time doing that.\n\nDoctor: Do you think that if you didn't repeat those actions, like rereading the label text or rearranging objects, something bad would happen?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I truly think something bad would happen… not exactly. It’s more that it feels wrong if I don’t. So I end up rereading the label, nudging the same objects around, checking the layout photos again, until it feels settled. It’s more… I reread the label or keep moving the objects and checking the layout until it feels settled. Mostly it just feels… settled for a bit after I reread it or move things enough. I just keep rereading the label or moving the objects until it feels settled. Mostly it just feels… settled for a bit after I reread it or move things enough. But I also know I’ve spent too much time on it.\n\nDoctor: Do you do these actions to feel less anxious?\n\nPatient: I guess… maybe a little, but it’s not really like I’m trying to calm myself down. I just keep rereading the label or moving the objects until it feels settled. Then I can stop, even though I know it was probably already fine and I’ve wasted time.\n\nDoctor: Are you doing these actions mostly to make the anxious feeling go away?\n\nPatient: Mostly it’s to make it feel… settled, I guess. I’ll reread the label or nudge things on the table, check the photos again, and part of me knows it’s probably already fine. But I keep going until that feeling eases enough to stop. It does cost me studio time.\n\nDoctor: Are the actions you take, like rereading the label or moving objects, primarily to reduce your anxiety?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, not exactly “anxiety,” maybe. It’s more that I reread the label text or nudge the objects, check the layout photos again, until it feels settled. I know it’s probably already fine, but I still end up losing studio time over it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to perform these actions, like rereading the label or moving objects, even if you don't want to?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess it does feel like I have to. I don’t really want to keep doing it, but I’ll reread the label or keep nudging the same things until it feels settled. Even when I know it’s probably fine.\n\nDoctor: Are your actions, like rereading the label or moving objects, intended to achieve a perfect result rather than to prevent something bad from happening?\n\nPatient: Yeah… more like that. I’m not usually thinking something terrible will happen. It’s just the label, or the objects on the table, or the layout photos don’t feel right yet, so I keep going until it feels settled. Even though I know it’s probably fine.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your actions, such as rereading the label or moving objects, are aimed at making everything perfect?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I’ll reread the label text, or move the same objects a little, check the layout photos again… just until it feels settled. I know it’s probably already fine, but I still end up doing it and losing time in the studio.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your actions are driven more by the need to get everything just right, rather than the fear of something going wrong if you don't?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it’s more that “just right” thing. I’ll reread the label, nudge things on the table, check the layout photos again… even though I know it’s probably fine. I just keep going until it feels settled, and then I’ve lost time.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your need to get everything just right is because you're afraid of making a mistake with the memorial piece?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think that’s part of it. With my aunt’s piece, I kept having these thoughts that if I put one photo in the wrong place, or picked the wrong color, it would mean I was dishonoring her somehow. Like it would say I didn’t love her enough. It felt awful, and I couldn’t really just dismiss it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're trying to avoid making a mistake with the memorial piece when you reread the label or move objects around?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I’ll reread the label and move things around a little, then check the layout photos again. It’s like I’m trying to make sure I didn’t get it wrong, even when I know it’s probably already fine. It just doesn’t feel settled yet.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're trying to avoid making a mistake with the memorial piece when you do these repetitive actions?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. I keep rereading the label, shifting the same objects a little, checking the photos again. It’s like I’m trying to catch some mistake, even though I know it’s probably fine. I just stop when it finally feels… settled, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Is your main goal with these actions to avoid doing something wrong or to make sure everything is perfect?\n\nPatient: More to make sure it’s perfect, I guess… or that it feels right. From the dialogue so far and your current candidates, ask the one follow-up question that best separates them.\n\n[Current Turn]\n19\n\n[Current Candidates]\nF42.2, F43.8\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I… I came because I’ve been having these unwanted thoughts while working on a memorial piece for my aunt. It feels like if I choose the wrong photo or color, somehow I’m dishonoring her, or it means I didn’t love her enough. I know it sounds like just creative stress, but I can’t really shake it.\n\nDoctor: Would you describe these thoughts as popping into your mind unexpectedly?\n\nPatient: Yes… they do. I’ll just be looking at the piece, trying to choose a photo or a color, and suddenly it’s like, what if this means I’m dishonoring her, or I didn’t love her enough. It feels unwanted, not like normal creative second-guessing.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself doing or thinking something to try to make these unwanted thoughts go away?\n\nPatient: Yes… I guess I do. I’ll reread the label text, move the same objects around on the table, check the layout photos again and again… just until it feels sort of “settled.” I know it’s probably already fine, but I can lose a lot of studio time doing that.\n\nDoctor: Do you think that if you didn't repeat those actions, like rereading the label text or rearranging objects, something bad would happen?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I truly think something bad would happen… not exactly. It’s more that it feels wrong if I don’t. So I end up rereading the label, nudging the same objects around, checking the layout photos again, until it feels settled. Even though part of me knows it’s probably already fine.\n\nDoctor: Do you think these actions, like rereading the label or rearranging objects, are specifically to prevent something from going wrong?\n\nPatient: Not really to prevent something, I don’t think. It’s more… I reread the label or keep moving the objects and checking the layout until it feels settled. I know it’s probably fine already, but I still end up doing it, and then I’ve lost studio time.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel less anxious after you've finished rereading the label or rearranging the objects?\n\nPatient: A little, I guess. Mostly it just feels… settled for a bit after I reread it or move things enough. But I also know I’ve spent too much time on it.\n\nDoctor: Do you do these actions to feel less anxious?\n\nPatient: I guess… maybe a little, but it’s not really like I’m trying to calm myself down. I just keep rereading the label or moving the objects until it feels settled. Then I can stop, even though I know it was probably already fine and I’ve wasted time.\n\nDoctor: Are you doing these actions mostly to make the anxious feeling go away?\n\nPatient: Mostly it’s to make it feel… settled, I guess. I’ll reread the label or nudge things on the table, check the photos again, and part of me knows it’s probably already fine. But I keep going until that feeling eases enough to stop. It does cost me studio time.\n\nDoctor: Are the actions you take, like rereading the label or moving objects, primarily to reduce your anxiety?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, not exactly “anxiety,” maybe. It’s more that I reread the label text or nudge the objects, check the layout photos again, until it feels settled. I know it’s probably already fine, but I still end up losing studio time over it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to perform these actions, like rereading the label or moving objects, even if you don't want to?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess it does feel like I have to. I don’t really want to keep doing it, but I’ll reread the label or keep nudging the same things until it feels settled. Even when I know it’s probably fine.\n\nDoctor: Are your actions, like rereading the label or moving objects, intended to achieve a perfect result rather than to prevent something bad from happening?\n\nPatient: Yeah… more like that. I’m not usually thinking something terrible will happen. It’s just the label, or the objects on the table, or the layout photos don’t feel right yet, so I keep going until it feels settled. Even though I know it’s probably fine.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your actions, such as rereading the label or moving objects, are aimed at making everything perfect?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I’ll reread the label text, or move the same objects a little, check the layout photos again… just until it feels settled. I know it’s probably already fine, but I still end up doing it and losing time in the studio.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your actions are driven more by the need to get everything just right, rather than the fear of something going wrong if you don't?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it’s more that “just right” thing. I’ll reread the label, nudge things on the table, check the layout photos again… even though I know it’s probably fine. I just keep going until it feels settled, and then I’ve lost time.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your need to get everything just right is because you're afraid of making a mistake with the memorial piece?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think that’s part of it. With my aunt’s piece, I kept having these thoughts that if I put one photo in the wrong place, or picked the wrong color, it would mean I was dishonoring her somehow. Like it would say I didn’t love her enough. It felt awful, and I couldn’t really just dismiss it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're trying to avoid making a mistake with the memorial piece when you reread the label or move objects around?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I’ll reread the label and move things around a little, then check the layout photos again. It’s like I’m trying to make sure I didn’t get it wrong, even when I know it’s probably already fine. It just doesn’t feel settled yet.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're trying to avoid making a mistake with the memorial piece when you do these repetitive actions?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. I keep rereading the label, shifting the same objects a little, checking the photos again. It’s like I’m trying to catch some mistake, even though I know it’s probably fine. I just stop when it finally feels… settled, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Is your main goal with these actions to avoid doing something wrong or to make sure everything is perfect?\n\nPatient: More to make sure it’s perfect, I guess… or that it feels right. I’ll reread the label, nudge the same objects, check the layout photos again. Part of me knows it’s already fine, but I keep going until it feels settled. It does eat up studio time.\n\nDoctor: Is it more important for you to avoid mistakes in the memorial piece or to make it perfectly right?\n\nPatient: I think… making it perfectly right. I’ll reread the label and nudge things around, check the photos again, even when I kind of know it’s fine. I just keep going until it feels settled.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

I just keep going until it feels settled, and then I’ve lost time.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your need to get everything just right is because you're afraid of making a mistake with the memorial piece?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think that’s part of it. With my aunt’s piece, I kept having these thoughts that if I put one photo in the wrong place, or picked the wrong color, it would mean I was dishonoring her somehow. Like it would say I didn’t love her enough. It felt awful, and I couldn’t really just dismiss it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're trying to avoid making a mistake with the memorial piece when you reread the label or move objects around?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I’ll reread the label and move things around a little, then check the layout photos again. It’s like I’m trying to make sure I didn’t get it wrong, even when I know it’s probably already fine. It just doesn’t feel settled yet.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're trying to avoid making a mistake with the memorial piece when you do these repetitive actions?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. I keep rereading the label, shifting the same objects a little, checking the photos again. It’s like I’m trying to catch some mistake, even though I know it’s probably fine. I just stop when it finally feels… settled, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Is your main goal with these actions to avoid doing something wrong or to make sure everything is perfect?\n\nPatient: More to make sure it’s perfect, I guess… or that it feels right. I’ll reread the label, nudge the same objects, check the layout photos again. Part of me knows it’s already fine, but I keep going until it feels settled. It does eat up studio time.\n\nDoctor: Is it more important for you to avoid mistakes in the memorial piece or to make it perfectly right?\n\nPatient: I think… making it perfectly right. I’ll reread the label and nudge things around, check the photos again, even when I kind of know it’s fine. I just keep going until it feels settled.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your need for everything to be 'just right' is more about achieving perfection or avoiding criticism?\n\nPatient: More perfection, I think.
